Normalizing flow regularization for photoacoustic tomography
Chao Wang, Alexandre H. Thiery

Abstract
Proper regularization is crucial in inverse problems to achieve high-quality reconstruction, even with an ill-conditioned measurement system. This is particularly true for three-dimensional photoacoustic tomography, which is computationally demanding and requires rapid scanning, often leading to incomplete measurements. Deep neural networks, known for their efficiency in handling big data, are anticipated to be adept at extracting underlying information from images sharing certain characteristics, such as specific types of natural or medical images. We introduce a Normalizing Flow Regularization method designed to reconstruct images from incomplete and noisy measurements. The method involves training a normalizing flow network to understand the statistical distribution of sample images by mapping them to Gaussian distributions. This well-trained network then acts as a regularization tool within a Bayesian inversion framework. Additionally, we explore the concept of adaptive regularization selection, providing theoretical proof of its admissibility. A significant challenge in three-dimensional image training is the extensive memory and computation requirements. We address this by training the normalizing flow model using only small-size images and applying a patch-based model for reconstructing larger images. Our approach is model-independent, allowing the reuse of a well-trained network as regularization for various imaging systems. Moreover, as a data-driven prior, Normalizing Flow Regularization effectively leverages the available dataset information, outperforming artificial priors. This advantage is demonstrated through numerical simulations of three-dimensional photoacoustic tomography under various conditions of sparsity, noise levels, and limited-view scenarios.



Aug 2022

1
Introduction

Photoacoustic tomography as a nonionizing imaging modality is promising for clinical applications
[
31
,
40
,
10
,
43
,
45
]
.
A short laser pulse illuminates a biological tissue, creating a small amount of heating throughout the tissue. This process leads to a thermoelastic expansion that generates a pressure wave that propagates through the object. Crucially, the initial pressure field created by the laser depends on the local properties of the biological tissue. Consequently, reconstructing the initial pressure field can be useful in obtaining diagnostic information of clinical interest.
The reconstruction is obtained through a set of transducers surrounding the biological tissue that can measure the time-dependent pressure field. The inverse problem that consists of reconstructing the initial pressure field from these measurements is the topic of this text.
For fast data acquisition, which is crucial in clinical settings, only incomplete and noisy measurements can be collected. Regularization methods are consequently of paramount importance for addressing the associated ill-conditioned inverse problem.
To exploit the statistical properties that scan of biological data are expected to satisfy (e.g. smooth background, sharp edges, the existence of low-rank or sparse structures), standard regularization techniques such as the use of
\ell_{p}
norms,
Total Variation
regularization, or nuclear norms have been widely adopted to improve the quality of reconstruction. However, these general and analytically tractable regularization methods are unlikely to efficiently exploit the structures that characterize the scans in specific subfields (e.g., brain fMRI scans have different statistical properties than chest X-ray scans). Using recent advances in generative modeling parametrized with deep neural networks, this article proposes a data-driven approach to construct appropriate regularization methods leveraging large data sets of complete measurements.

Deep learning approaches are already playing a crucial role in image reconstruction
[
13
,
42
,
32
,
4
,
30
]
. This includes post-processing-based methods, model-motivated models, and deep learning-based regularization. Post-processing-based methods are models that can transform a rough reconstruction into a more accurate one: once the post-processing model is fitted to data, which can be a computationally intensive procedure, deploying the model is generally computationally cheap and straightforward to implement. However, this class of methods generally relies on the existence of a large
training
dataset of pairs of input data and the corresponding accurate reconstruction. In medical settings, constructing such datasets can be difficult due to the associated costs and clinical constraints. Furthermore, post-processing methods can generally only be deployed for data obtained with the same imaging system as the one used for creating the training dataset.
Model-motivated methods are a class of algorithms that learn (part of) an iterative optimization process for reconstruction purposes: this can lead to important computational savings while maintaining the reconstruction accuracy
[
12
,
15
,
8
,
9
,
21
,
3
,
2
,
46
]
.
Finally, deep learning-based regularization approaches consist of using training data to build appropriate regularization terms
[
4
,
35
,
51
]
; the methods proposed in this text belong to this class of methods.

In photoacoustic tomography, deep learning methods already produce remarkable results
[
23
,
17
,
25
,
38
]
. For example, Convolutional Neural Networks
Convolutional Neural Network
have been used for designing post-processing methods to improve the accuracy of rough reconstructions obtained with standard methods such as
universal back-projection

[
47
]
or adjoint-based approaches
[
18
,
19
,
6
,
5
,
41
,
20
,
25
,
28
]
.
For model-motivated methods, due to the computationally demanding nature of estimating the forward and backward processes, each iteration is typically trained separately
[
24
]
.
In
[
29
,
7
,
35
]
, deep neural networks are utilized as regularizers to remove artifacts, and a complete convergence analysis of the proposed algorithms is also established.
These recently developed methodologies demonstrate the potential of data-driven approaches in the image reconstruction of photoacoustic tomography
photoacoustic tomography
. It is important to note that, unlike in
Computer Tomography
or
Magnetic Resonance Imaging
where large datasets are widely available, obtaining large datasets of paired images for emerging imaging technologies like photoacoustic tomography remains challenging. Therefore, for photoacoustic tomography, it is crucial to design methods that (1) do not rely on datasets of paired images and (2) are independent of the measurement system and noise statistics.

In this article, we propose the use of a normalizing flow
[
26
]
as a regularizer to address the ill-posedness in image reconstruction with limited-view measurements. A trained normalizing flow is designed to map a distribution of images (i.e., training data) to a tractable
base distribution
, typically chosen as an isotropic Gaussian. This procedure offers a data-driven approach to building a prior distribution, which can subsequently be used within a Bayesian inversion framework
[
33
,
39
]
. Compared to more standard algorithms that typically rely on a small number of hand-crafted features and generic regularization terms, this data-driven approach more effectively leverages the statistical properties of the distribution of images being reconstructed. Additionally, the method is agnostic to the imaging system and noise statistics; only a dataset of
ground truth
images is necessary. Once trained, the model can be applied within different measurement settings.

In this paper, we describe the
Normalizing Flow Regularization
method, a Bayesian inversion algorithm for three-dimensional photoacoustic tomography. Here, a normalizing flow is used to construct a prior distribution of images using a training dataset of clear images. Our contributions are as follows:

•

We show that normalizing flow successfully learns the distribution of the training data and performs effectively as a regularizer within iterative methods.
Normalizing Flow Regularization
method achieves competitive reconstruction compared to state-of-the-art methods such as
Total Variation
regularization iterative methods and
Convolutional Neural Network
post-processing method.

•

We investigate the tuning of the regularization parameter. We provide a rule for adaptively selecting the regularization parameter and prove its admissibility.

•

To mitigate the computational challenges associated with processing large three-dimensional images, we develop a
patch-based
approach
[
27
]
that involves randomly selecting several patches across the three-dimensional image.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we describe the Bayesian framework for solving the inverse problem of
photoacoustic tomography
. Some background material on
photoacoustic tomography
is contained in Section
2.1
, and normalizing flow models are introduced in Section
2.2
. The well-posedness of
Normalizing Flow Regularization
is analyzed in Section
3.1
and
3.2
using
Glow
as an example. A strategy to select the regularization parameter is proposed and studied in Section
3.3
. The corresponding algorithm is described in Section
4
: the adaptive algorithm to select the regularization parameter is described and analyzed in Section
4.1
, and the patch-based approach is discussed in Section
4.2
. Section
5
compares in different sparsity and noise levels the
Normalizing Flow Regularization
with standard
Total Variation
methods and a
Convolutional Neural Network
post-processing methods. Section
6
concludes the article.

2
Bayesian inverse problems in
photoacoustic tomography

2.1
Inverse problem in
photoacoustic tomography

As explained in the introduction, in three-dimensional
photoacoustic tomography
, the illumination of a biological tissue
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
with a laser creates a spatially inhomogeneous pressure field that depends on the local biological properties of the tissue. The initial pressure field
p_{0}(\bm{r})\equiv p(\bm{r},t=0)
, for spatial coordinate
\bm{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then propagates through the tissues before being measured by a set of
M\geq 1
transducers surrounding the tissue. In other words, the transducers measure the time-dependent pressure field
p(\bm{r},t)
for
t\in[0,T]
at a finite set of spatial locations
\{\bm{r}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
located on the boundary of
\Omega
.
Assuming that the biological tissue is acoustically homogeneous (i.e., the acoustic velocity
c_{0}
is constant throughout the medium) and acoustically linear and non-attenuating on the spatial and time-relevant scales, as is approximately the case in most clinically relevant situations, the evolution of the pressure field
p(\bm{r},t)
is described by a constant coefficient wave equation,

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial^{2}_{tt}\,p(\bm{r},t)=c_{0}^{2}\,\Delta p(\bm{r},t)&&\qquad(\bm{r},t)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,T],\\
&p(\bm{r},0)=p_{0}(\bm{r}),&&\qquad\bm{r}\in\Omega,\\
&p(\bm{r},0)=0,&&\qquad\bm{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}/\Omega,\\
&\frac{\partial p}{\partial t}(r,0)=0,&&\qquad\bm{r}\in\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

(1)

This initial value problem for the wave equation is well studied, and the pressure field at time
t\in[0,T]
is given by Kirchhoff’s formula. We have that

p(\bm{r},t)=\partial_{t}\,\Big\{c_{0}\,t\times(\mathcal{A}p_{0})(\bm{r},c_{0}\,t)\Big\},

(2)

where, for any
\bm{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and radius
R>0
, the quantity
(\mathcal{A}p_{0})(\bm{r},R)
is the average of the initial pressure field
p_{0}
on the sphere centered at
\bm{r}
with radius
R
,

(\mathcal{A}p_{0})(\bm{r},R)\equiv\int_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{v}$}}}\in\mathbb{S}_{2}}p_{0}(\bm{r}+R\,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{v}$}}})\,\sigma(d{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{v}$}}}),

(3)

with
\sigma(d{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{v}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{v}$}}})
the uniform probability distribution on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}_{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The averaging operator
\mathcal{A}
is related to the Radon-transform that has been extensively studied in computational tomography. After discretization, this linear inverse problem can be formulated as

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}

(4)

for a vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
representing the discretization of the unknown initial acoustic pressure field
p_{0}(\bm{r})
and a vector
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}}}
that represents the collected data contaminated by additive noise
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}}}
. In the remainder of this text, we assume that the additive error term
\textstyle{\eta}
follows a centered Gaussian distribution with covariance matrix
\Gamma_{\eta}
although the our proposed methodology readily generalizes to other noise distributions. Note that the matrix
\textstyle{F}
that represents the discretization of the forward operator (
1
) is typically extremely ill-conditioned and is never actually computed or stored in memory.

Combining a prior distribution with density
\pi_{\mbox{\tiny prior}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})
(with respect to the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
) that encodes the probabilistic assumptions on
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
with the probabilistic model described in Equation (
4
) leads to the posterior distribution
\pi_{\mbox{\tiny post}}(d{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})
whose density (with respect to the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
) reads

\displaystyle\pi_{\mbox{\tiny post}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})=\tfrac{1}{Z}\,\pi_{\mbox{\tiny prior}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\,\exp{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}_{\Gamma^{-1}_{\eta}}\right\}}

(5)

for a normalization constant
Z=\int\,\pi_{\mbox{\tiny prior}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\,\exp{\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}_{\Gamma^{-1}_{\eta}}\right\}}\,d{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}
. We have used the standard weighted norm notation
\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}_{\Gamma^{-1}_{\eta}}\equiv\langle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}},\Gamma^{-1}_{\eta}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\rangle
. In this text, we are primarily interested in leveraging data to design an appropriate prior distribution
\pi_{\mbox{\tiny prior}}
that encodes as faithfully as possible the statistical properties of the discretized initial pressure field
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
and compute the
maximum a posteriori
estimate
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\textbf{MAP}}=\argmax\pi_{\mbox{\tiny post}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})
. Indeed, the
maximum a posteriori
estimate
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\textbf{MAP}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
can also be expressed as the minimizer of the objective function,

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\;\mapsto\;\frac{1}{2}\,\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}_{\Gamma^{-1}_{\eta}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})

(6)

where the negative log-prior density
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\equiv-\log\pi_{\mbox{\tiny prior}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})
takes the role of a regularization term. The negative log-likelihood
\frac{1}{2}\,\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}_{\Gamma^{-1}_{\eta}}
is a data-misfit term.
Furthermore, we only consider the case where the covariance of the additive noise
\eta
is isotropic with variance
\lambda>0
, i.e. we have
\Gamma_{\eta}\equiv\lambda\,\mathbf{I}_{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}}}
, although more complex covariance structures would be straightforward to accommodate with the methods described in this text. The
maximum a posteriori
estimate
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\textbf{MAP}}
can consequently be expressed as the minimizer of the objective

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda)\;\equiv\;\frac{1}{2}\,\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}+\lambda\,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}).

(7)

The automated tuning of the parameter
\lambda>0
is important in practice and adaptive strategies are presented and theoretically analyzed in Sections
3.3
and
4.1
.

2.2
Normalizing flow-based image prior

In order to design a sensible prior distribution that translates the statistical assumptions on the discretization
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
of the initial pressure field
p_{0}
, a natural approach consists in fitting a statistical model to a large dataset of ground truth samples
\mathcal{D}\equiv\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{D}|}
. In this text, we illustrate the proposed method on
Computer Tomography
data. For a family of probability densities
\pi_{\theta}
on
\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
parametrized by a parameter
\theta\in\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}^{D_{\Theta}}
, this program can be carried out by
maximum likelihood estimation
, i.e. minimization of the negative log-likelihood function

\displaystyle\theta\mapsto\sum_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}-\log\pi_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i}).

Indeed, density estimation in high-dimensional scenarios faces significant challenges. Moreover, designing flexible parametrized densities in
\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
to accurately model the statistics of biological tissue scans is not straightforward. Here, we leverage
Normalizing Flow
parametrized with 3D
Convolutional Neural Network
since this type of neural architectures have the correct inductive biases for efficiently modeling spatially extended samples with local correlation structures. A
Normalizing Flow
model on
\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
defines a family of densities
\pi_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})
by leveraging a family of diffeomorphisms, i.e. continuously differentiable bijective mappings, and a base probability density
\pi_{B}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}})
on
\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
. More precisely, for a (generative) diffeomorphism
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}}}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
and its inverse
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}}}_{\theta}\circ{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\qquad\text{for all}\quad({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}},\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}\times\Theta,

the distribution
\pi_{\theta}
is defined as the push-forward of the base distribution
\pi_{B}
through the generative mapping
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}}}_{\theta}
. For any (bounded) measurable function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}\to\mathbb{R}
we have that

\displaystyle\int\varphi({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\,\pi_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\,d{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}=\int\varphi[{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}}}_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}})]\,\pi_{B}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}})\,d{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}}.

A sample
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\sim\pi_{\theta}
can be constructed by first sampling from the base distribution
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}}\sim\pi_{B}
and then set

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\;=\;{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{G}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}}}_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}}).

The mapping
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}
is
normalizing
in the sense that it maps the distribution
\pi_{\theta}
to the base distribution
\pi_{B}
. The density
\pi_{\theta}
reads

\displaystyle\pi_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\;=\;\pi_{B}[{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})]\times\left|\det\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\right|

(8)

where
\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})
denotes the Jacobian matrix of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}
at
\textstyle{x}
. In practice, the normalizing mapping
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}
is obtained by composing
K\geq 1
simpler diffeomorphism mappings,

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}\;\equiv\;{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,K}\circ\cdots\circ{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,2}\circ{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,1}.

In this text and as is standard in applications, the base distribution
\pi_{B}
is chosen as the standard centered Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I})
in
\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
so that
-\log\pi_{B}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}})=\frac{1}{2}\,\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{z}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{z}$}}}\|^{2}+\textrm{(constant)}
. Importantly, the mappings
\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
are constructed so that the Jacobian determinants
\det(\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,k}})
are straightforward to compute. This implies that

\displaystyle-\log\pi_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})=\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\|^{2}-\sum_{k=1}^{K}\log\left|\det[\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,k}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k})]\right|+\textrm{(constant)}

(9)

with
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{1}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k+1}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{k}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k})
for
1\leq k\leq K-1
. Section
3.1
describes more precisely the neural parametrization of the transformations
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,k}
. The parameter
\theta\in\Theta
that describes the neural weights parametrizing the normalizing function
\textstyle{N}
is obtained by maximum likelihood estimation,

\displaystyle\theta_{\star}\;=\;\argmin_{\theta\in\Theta}\;{\left\{\;\theta\mapsto\sum_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i}\in\mathcal{D}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i})\|^{2}-\log\left|\det[\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i})]\right|\Big)\;\right\}}.

(10)

Once the neural parameter
\theta_{\star}\in\Theta
is obtained, this defines a data-driven regularization functional,

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\equiv\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\|^{2}-\log\left|\det[\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})]\right|=-\log\pi_{\theta_{\star}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})+\textrm{(constant)}

that approximates, up to an irrelevant additive constant, the negative log-density of the ground truth samples
\mathcal{D}\equiv\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{D}|}
.
The
maximum a posteriori
estimate
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\textbf{MAP}}
of the discretized initial pressure field
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}}
can consequently be obtained by minimizing the loss

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda)\;\equiv\;\frac{1}{2}\,\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}+\lambda\,\Big(\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\|^{2}-\log\left|\det[\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})]\right|\Big).

(11)

This minimization can be carried out with standard adaptive stochastic-gradient-descent methods.

3
Analysis of normalizing flow-based regularization (NFR)

In this section, the well-posedness (existence, stability, and convergence) of
Normalizing Flow Regularization
is discussed. For concreteness, the
Glow
neural architecture
[
26
]
is used in Section
3.1
and
3.2
, although our proposed method is agnostic to the choice of neural architecture. Importantly, an adaptive tuning strategy to select the regularization parameter is presented and analyzed in Section
3.3
.

3.1
Glow
model

Given dataset
\mathcal{D}\equiv\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{D}|}
, the normalizing flow
Glow
model can be used to for density estimation; the parameters
\theta_{\star}
of the
Glow
model are obtained through Maximum Likelihood Estimation as described in Equation (
10
).
Empirical evidences show that
Glow
can lead to significant improvements when compared to other standard flow models such as
NICE

[
22
]
or
RealNVP

[
14
]
. A
Glow
model
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}
with parameter
\theta
consists of multiple steps of flow
f_{\theta,k}\ (k=1,2,\dots,K)
that are connected by squeeze layers mappings
q_{\theta,k}\ (k=1,2,\dots,K)
and split layers
h_{\theta,k}\ (k=1,2,\dots,K)
. The model can be summarized as follows,

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta}\;\equiv\;{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,K}\circ\cdots\circ{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,2}\circ{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,1},

(12)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta,k}\equiv h_{\theta,k}\circ f_{\theta,k}\circ q_{\theta,k}\ (k=1,2,\dots,K),

where
h_{\theta,K}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{I}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{I}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{I}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{I}$}}}
.
In (
12
),
f^{1}_{\theta,k}
is an activation normalization layer,
f^{2}_{\theta,k}
is an invertible
1\times 1
convolution and
f^{3}_{\theta,k}
is an affine coupling layer. We have

\displaystyle f_{\theta,k}=f^{3}_{\theta,k}\odot f^{2}_{\theta,k}\odot f^{1}_{\theta,k},

(13a)

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,1}=f^{1}_{\theta,k}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,0})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{s}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{s}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{s}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{s}$}}}_{\theta,k}\odot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,0}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{b}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{b}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{b}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{b}$}}}_{\theta,k},

(13b)

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2}=f^{2}_{\theta,k}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,1})=\bm{W}_{\theta,k}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,1},

(13c)

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,3}=f^{3}_{\theta,k}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2})=\begin{pmatrix}g^{1}_{\theta,k}\odot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{a}^{k,2}+g^{2}_{\theta,k}\\
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2}_{b}\end{pmatrix},

(13d)

where the inputs of
f_{\theta,k}^{1}
,
f_{\theta,k}^{2}
and
f_{\theta,k}^{3}
are denoted by
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,0}
,
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,1}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2}
with
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,0}=q_{\theta,k}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k})
and
h_{\theta,k}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,3})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k+1}
. Note that
\bm{W}_{\theta,k}
in (
13c
) is an invertible matrix product. Affine coupling layer (
13d
) acts on the two halves
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2}_{a}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2}_{b}
that are splits along the channel of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2}
. The quantities
g^{1}_{\theta,k}
and
g^{2}_{\theta,k}
are defined as

\displaystyle[\log g^{1}_{\theta,k},g^{2}_{\theta,k}]\;=\;\texttt{NN}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{k,2}_{b}),

where
\log g^{2}_{\theta,k}
and
g^{1}_{\theta,k}
are the two parts of the output of the network
NN
, separated by channels. The neural network
NN
consists of three convolutional layers and nonlinear activation functions
RELU
. The reader is referred to
[
26
]
for details.

3.2
Well-posedness of the regularization

Once the parameters
\theta_{\star}
of the
Glow
model are obtained, these parameters are fixed. The reconstruction of the initial pressure field is then carried out by minimizing the following regularized objective,

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}})&\;\equiv\;\frac{1}{2}\,\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|^{2}_{2}+\lambda\,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\\
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})&\;\equiv\;\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\|^{2}-\log\left|\det[\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})]\right|,\end{aligned}\right.

(14)

where parameter
\lambda>0
quantifies the amount of regularization
To proceed with the analysis of this regularized objective, any solution
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{x}}
that satisfies

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{+})=\inf{\left\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\;:\;{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}\right\}}.

(15)

is referred to as a
deep-prior solution
. In other words, among all the solutions to the equation
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}
, deep-prior solutions are the ones that minimize the regularization functional
\textstyle{R}
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Consider the regularized objective (
14
). The following properties hold:

1.

Existence:
for any
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}}}
and
\lambda>0
, there exists at least one minimizer of the regularized objective
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\mapsto\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}})
;

2.

Stability:
for any
\lambda>0
and sequence
\{({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{k})\}_{k\geq 0}
such that
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{k}\rightarrow{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D}_{y}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k}\in\argmin\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{k})
, there exists a subsequence of
\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
that converges to a minimizer of the regularized objective
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\mapsto\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}})
.

3.

Convergence:
for
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}\in X
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}^{*}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}
, let a sequence
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{k}
satisfies
\left\lVert{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}^{*}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{k}\right\rVert_{2}\leq\delta_{k}\rightarrow 0(k\rightarrow\infty)
, and regularization parameters satisfy
\lambda_{k}\rightarrow 0
and
\delta_{k}^{2}/\lambda_{k}\rightarrow 0
, then the sequence of the minimizers of
\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda_{k},{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{k})
will at least has a subsequence that converges to a minimizer of
\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda=0,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}})
. The limits of convergent subsequences are deep-prior solutions of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}
if the solutions of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}
exist.

Proof.

The forward operator
\textstyle{F}
is a finite-dimensional linear operator from
\mathbb{R}^{D_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}
to
\mathbb{R}^{D_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}}
. Furthermore, the regularization functional
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})=\frac{1}{2}\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\|^{2}-\log\left|\det[\jac_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{N}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{N}$}}}_{\theta_{\star}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})]\right|
is continuous with
\lim_{\|{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\|\to\infty}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}(x)=\infty
.
Consequently, the existence, stability and convergence properties of the regularized functional (
14
) follow from
[
37
]
(Chapter 5) and
[
29
]
(Theorem 2.6).

∎

Remark
.

A similar study of regularization with deep neural artchitecture can be found in
[
29
]
. Weak convergence is discussed for sequentially lower semi-continuous regularizers
\textstyle{R}
defined on infinite-dimensional space and conditions for strong convergence to hold are discussed.

3.3
Parameter selection strategy

This section describes an adaptation strategy for setting the regularization parameter
\lambda>0
.
Its admissibility is proved in Theorem
3.3
while its existence is proved in Theorem
3.7
.
For any sample
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}
, it is assumed that the noise is
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}}
, and the observation is
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}}
. According to Theorem
3.1
, a minimizer of
\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}})
exists and it is denoted as

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\lambda}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}}\in\argmin{\left\{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\;\mapsto\;\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}$}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\lambda\,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\right\}}.

(16)

Since the noise level is unknown in applications of practical interest, the regularization parameter
\lambda
can be difficult to choose. We propose to set
\lambda=\lambda({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}})
chosen so that

{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\lambda}^{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}})={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{R}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{R}$}}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}).

(17)

This strategy is referred to as
regularizer consistency
in the rest of this article.

Definition 3.2
.

For the inversion from
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}:={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}+{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}
to
\textstyle{x}
by the minimization (
16
), a regularization strategy
\lambda=\lambda({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}})
is called admissible if when
\left\lVert{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\right\rVert_{2}\rightarrow 0
, any minimizer
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\lambda}^{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}
satisfies

\left\lVert\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{null}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}})^{\bot}}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\lambda}^{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}-{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}})\right\rVert_{2}\rightarrow 0,

(18)

where
\textrm{null}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}})^{\bot}
is the orthogonal complement of the nullspace of
\textstyle{F}
, and
\mathcal{P}
is the projection operator. If
\textstyle{F}
is injective, the projection operator in (
18
) can be removed.

The following theorem establishes that the adaptive rule described by Equation (
17
) is admissible.

Theorem 3.3
.

Assuming
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\in\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{D_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}}
and
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}
, if for any
\textstyle{\eta}
with
\left\lVert{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\right\rVert_{2}<\left\lVert{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}\right\rVert_{2}
, there exists
\lambda
such that there exist
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}_{\lambda}^{{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}}\in\argmin_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}\mathcal{L}({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}};\lambda,{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{y}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{y}$}}}_{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}})
satisfying (
17
), then
(
17
)
is an admissible rule. Furthermore, if
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}
is a deep-prior solution of
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{F}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{F}$}}}{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{x}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{x}$}}}^{*}
, when
{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}={\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}_{k}\rightarrow 0
and
\lambda_{k}=\lambda({\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle{\eta}$}}}_{k})
such that (
17
) holds, there exist accumulation points of minimizers
